# 01 · Foundation decision: what OneMind OS is built on

**Owner:** DEC, revision 4 by DEC4 (synthesis of W2–W8, W18, W19's DC-16, the Tier A and Tier C teardowns, eighteen VM lab spikes and 151 refuter/steelman reports, 29 of them new in rev. 4) · **Date:** 2026-11-11 (revision 4; revisions 2 and 3 earlier the same day, revision 1 on 2026-11-10) · **Status:** Tier A decision, **revision 4** (DEC4, 2026-11-11): the S1 base-OS result is re-derived under the frozen scorecard protocol; ready for the founder's review, with three new founder decisions (Z-38…Z-40)
**What revision 4 changed:** the rev-3 three-way pairwise tie-break, which kept Debian, was a rule written after the scores were known and never logged, so it is **withdrawn** [C-PROTO4-018]. Under the frozen rules, with two evidence corrections applied equally to every candidate (the Secure Boot lab now counts for every RAUC stack), the card is **Ubuntu 26.04 65 · bootc 64 · Debian 63**. The locked top-two tie-break gives S1 to **Ubuntu 26.04 + RAUC (6–4 over bootc)**: fragile (11 of 14 runs), and CONDITIONAL on Canonical's permission (Z-01). The outcome of each pending test is pre-registered: W2-HW-3 failing gives Debian; a Canonical refusal gives bootc; and (FIX4, after three refuters attacked the rev-4 result) an Ubuntu Secure Boot leg failing gives bootc (§3.1.6 h, §5.1.2). DC-12, DC-20 and E10 now have dedicated refuters, eight borrowed DCs have round-4 refuters, an arm64 RAUC lab and a RAUC 1.15.2 Secure Boot re-run passed, and citations carry CIT4's source labels. **ADR-001 D2 (Debian core) is no longer the scorecard's result** (§7.3).
**What revision 3 changed:** the fully cited scorecard ([`evidence/scorecard-rev3.md`](evidence/scorecard-rev3.md)) makes **RHEL-family bootc the raw S1 leader (64.0)** on one documentation-only Pi 5 cell, so the S1 tie-break is re-run with all three bases as finalists (§3.1.5); Ubuntu 26.04 and Mender now have their **own lab legs** under the same pre-registered battery (critic pass-2 N7), so the "only Debian/RAUC was tested" rows are dropped and both tie-breaks are recounted; RAUC 1.15.2 passed the real-k3s battery and Debian's 1.13 was found CVE-patched but **broken on kernels ≥ 6.19** (§4.2); a search for a shipped Debian/Ubuntu + RAUC appliance found **none** (N9, §9.1); DC-20 was measured (§6.2); 19 more DC refuters, 10 more DC-16 refuters and two new steelmen (embedded etcd, CRA notified body) are answered (§5, §6). **Both leads survive, narrower; no ADR-001 core element is overturned** (§7, final).
**What revision 2 changed:** every P0 conclusion now has at least three independent refuters (§5); 17 decision-critical refuters and 15 DC-16 refuters were added (§5.9, §5.10); seven independent runner-up steelmen are summarised (§6.5–6.11); two new labs ran (RAUC with a real k3s gate, and RAUC under enforced Secure Boot, §10); DC-16 is decided (§4.8). Eleven revision-1 positions changed and are listed in §7.1. No core element of ADR-001 was overturned, but the base-OS and update-engine leads are now **thin**.
**Decides:** ADR-001 D1–D6 · decisions S1–S5 of the frozen scorecard ([`evidence/scorecard-weights.md`](evidence/scorecard-weights.md) v1.0, unchanged) · the 24 decision-critical questions ([`evidence/decision-critical.md`](evidence/decision-critical.md))
**Inputs:** [`03`](03-base-os.md) · [`04`](04-image-install-updates-rollback.md) · [`05`](05-kubernetes-cluster-conversion-nvidia.md) · [`06`](06-backups-recovery.md) · [`07`](07-security-compliance.md) · [`08`](08-robotics-drones-physical-ai.md) · [`09`](09-iot-mesh-offgrid-tak.md) · [`28`](28-KERNEL-IMAGE-OWNERSHIP-AND-DEV-TOOLCHAIN.md) · [`02-competitors/`](02-competitors/) · [`evidence/adversarial/`](evidence/adversarial/) · [`evidence/lab/`](evidence/lab/)
**Claims:** every load-bearing sentence cites a claim ID that exists in `evidence/claims/*.csv`. New facts verified by this agent are `C-DEC-001…015` (rev. 3 added 011–013 for N9 and 014–015 for two refuter sources that were cited under colliding IDs; see `sources/DEC.md`) ([`evidence/claims/DEC.csv`](evidence/claims/DEC.csv), sources in [`evidence/sources/DEC.md`](evidence/sources/DEC.md)). Counter-arguments in §5 are summaries of the refuter files, which carry their own sources.
**Serves requirements:** RS-1, RS-2, RS-4, P-1, P-4, R-1, R-2, R-3, M-2, M-4, M-6, M-7, M-8, N-1, N-2, N-4, N-6, N-7, N-8, N-9, H-1, H-3, P-3, P-5, P-7, P-8, P-9, U-4, AI-4, AI-5, AI-7, AI-9, UX-6, A-1, A-5, F-1, A-6, AI-3, AI-8, F-2, F-3, F-4, F-5, F-6, F-7, F-8, F-11, F-12, F-14, F-15, D-001, D-002

> ### Explain it like I'm the founder
> You asked one question: **what should OneMind OS stand on for the next 10 years?** Here is the answer, layer by layer, in plain words.
>
> 1. **Base OS (revision 4): under the contest rules we locked before scoring, Ubuntu 26.04 LTS wins, narrowly and with conditions.** The three serious bases now score within 2 points of each other (Ubuntu 65, bootc 64, Debian 63). The rules call that a tie between the top two, Ubuntu and bootc, and the locked tie-break evidence gives it to **Ubuntu, 6 rows to 4** (§3.1.6). Revision 3 had kept Debian by bringing it in as a third finalist. That step was a rule written after the scores were known, so revision 4 takes it back [C-PROTO4-018]. Ubuntu's win carries conditions. Canonical must allow OneMind to ship a modified Ubuntu image, or OneMind must strip the trademarks and rebuild the binaries (Z-01) [C-W2-050]. Jetson still runs NVIDIA's own Ubuntu 24.04, a second release. Two answers can still change the winner, and we wrote down in advance what each one means. If bootc fails on a real Pi 5 (W2-HW-3), the rules give **Debian**. If Canonical says no and a debranded rebuild is too costly, they give **bootc**. Until then the image is built from one definition in a Debian and an Ubuntu version, so that work is not wasted. bootc would be a different build, which is why the Pi 5 test now runs before week 4. **You can still choose Debian yourself**; Z-38 sets out the case for and against, and the choice is then logged as yours.
> 2. **Fedora IoT (your question): a profile, not the base.** Its bootc lineage did exactly what we want in our lab (it refused a broken update and rolled back by itself [C-W2-054]), and Red Hat has the industrial certificates [C-W2-042]. But on the Pi 5 it is still experimental [C-W2-018] and each Fedora release lives about 13 months [C-W2-015]. So it becomes the **Industrial/Enterprise profile** later, with Fedora IoT as our early-warning "canary".
> 3. **Updates: two copies of the OS (A/B), engine changed from Rugix to RAUC.** Rugix is excellent but is written by essentially one person (303 of 304 commits in a year [C-W3-001]); RAUC runs Home Assistant OS. RAUC's own core is mostly two engineers at one company [C-REF-W3-longevity-001], so its lead is narrower than it first looked, and on corrected scores **Mender is now one point ahead of RAUC (62 vs 61), a tie** (§3.2). RAUC keeps the lead on the tie-break: it is the only engine our lab has run with real Kubernetes **and** under Secure Boot, its delta updates are free, and it can revoke keys. Both labs passed every test [C-LAB-rauc-k3s-gate-006…010, C-LAB-secureboot-rauc-012…016]. The disk layout stays engine-neutral, so we can switch engines later with an update, not a reflash.
> 4. **"Healthy" must mean healthy *now*.** Our lab found a trap nobody documents: after an update, Kubernetes shows the *previous* boot's "all good" status. A naive check committed an update whose Kubernetes never started, first with Rugix [C-LAB-debian-rugix-023] and again with RAUC and real k3s [C-LAB-rauc-k3s-gate-008]. Our gate checks fresh signals only, and it caught every broken update.
> 4b. **Secure Boot works with this layout, today, in emulation.** Our own keys, signed kernels per slot, encrypted data that unlocks by itself after updates and rollbacks, and refuses when Secure Boot is switched off [C-LAB-secureboot-rauc-012…016]. One gap: Secure Boot accepts any *old* signed kernel, so OneMind needs an anti-downgrade rule [C-LAB-secureboot-rauc-018].
> 5. **Kubernetes: k3s on every box with at least 4 GB of RAM and an SSD; smaller gadgets join over NATS.** k3s costs about 1 GB of RAM in our lab [C-LAB-k3s-footprint-restore-007]. Robots and field kits that someone could steal run only the k3s *agent*, never the part that holds every password [C-REF-W4-security-008]. RKE2 is kept for the day a regulated or big-GPU customer needs it.
> 6. **Backups: restic, plus a correct way to back up k3s's database on a single box.** ADR-001's "k3s etcd-snapshot" simply fails on a single box [C-W5-001]; the right method restored a whole box onto a fresh machine in our lab [C-LAB-k3s-footprint-restore-011].
> 7. **Security and the law:** decide the disk layout *now* so Secure Boot and disk encryption can be switched on later without wiping boxes, and be ready for the EU Cyber Resilience Act, which expects security updates to install automatically by default [C-W6-008]. That last point collides with "never auto-install" and is your call.
> 8. **Plugins (DC-16): ten "sockets" in release 1, and they must be locked.** Apps, widgets, AI tools and automations live in Kubernetes. Drivers and OS profiles need a standard Linux feature (system extensions) that lays signed add-ons over the read-only OS [C-W19-006…009]. The refuters showed that, out of the box, this feature also accepts *unsigned* add-ons [C-REF-W19-security-004], so OneMind must switch that off before any third party can plug in.
> 9. **The Ubuntu question is bigger than we thought.** Our Jetson robots run NVIDIA's Ubuntu, and that is also a *modified Ubuntu*, so Canonical's permission question applies to them too [C-REF-DC-cost-007 (SECONDARY: NVIDIA staff on forum), C-LIC-011]. That is why we now also test a Jetson image that does not depend on Ubuntu at all (Yocto meta-tegra) in the first 180 days.
>
> **Revision 3, in plain words (the final pass of this run):**
> 10. **The fair fight happened.** The reviewer said Debian and RAUC won partly because they were the only ones we tested. So Ubuntu 26.04 and Mender each sat the exact same exam. **Both passed** [C-LAB-ubuntu-2604-leg-011, C-LAB-mender-008…014]. Those "lab" points are gone, and we recounted. **Debian still wins the base, and RAUC still wins the updater, but by less.** Mender's one real weakness in the lab: it gives a new version only **one** boot to prove itself, where RAUC gives three [C-LAB-mender-013].
> 11. **A new raw leader, not a new decision.** When every score had to carry a source, Red Hat's family (bootc) jumped to first place on paper (64 vs Ubuntu 62 vs Debian 60), because AlmaLinux now documents *experimental* Raspberry Pi 5 images [C-SCORE-025]. The rules call that a tie. Revision 3 then compared the three head-to-head and gave Debian the win. **Revision 4 withdrew that comparison**, because it was a rule invented after the scores were known (item 15). One test on a real Pi 5 (W2-HW-3) settles whether bootc's paper lead is real.
> 12. **RAUC's version problem is solved, and a hidden one found.** Debian quietly fixed RAUC's 2026 security bug [C-LAB-rauc-1152-005], and the newest RAUC (1.15.2) passed every test [C-LAB-rauc-1152-011]. But Debian's old RAUC **cannot install any update on a newer Linux kernel** (6.19 or later) [C-LAB-ubuntu-2604-leg-010]. So OneMind builds and ships its own RAUC, as Home Assistant does.
> 13. **Nobody ships exactly our combination in production.** We found no field-proven product running Debian or Ubuntu with RAUC, and none with Kubernetes on top [C-DEC-013, UNVERIFIED: negative search]. The closest is IronFlockOS, a pre-1.0 open-source industrial OS on Debian 13 + RAUC + Docker that has not yet passed hardware acceptance [C-CIT4-001, C-CIT4-002]. Each piece is proven somewhere (Debian in umbrelOS, RAUC in Home Assistant and ZimaOS, k3s in Olares), but putting them together is our own work, so our lab results and the hardware tests are the only proof. That is why the disk layout stays engine-neutral.
> 14. **Building from source like Home Assistant does is not a security shortcut.** We built the same tiny OS both ways: from source it is 4–6 times smaller, but has only 7–18% fewer known holes, because most holes live in the Linux kernel either way [C-LAB-minimal-image-dc20-011]. Debian stays.
>
> **Revision 4, in plain words:**
> 15. **We followed our own rules.** The critic and three new refuters found that revision 3 changed the tie-break rule after seeing the scores. Revision 4 applies the rule as it was locked. Under it, **Ubuntu 26.04 wins the base-OS contest**: it beats bootc 6 rows to 4 on the decision-critical evidence. It wins in 11 of 14 "shake the weights" runs, so the result is *fragile*, not settled (§3.1.6).
> 16. **Two corrections, applied to everyone.** Our Secure Boot lab now counts for every option that uses RAUC (Debian, Ubuntu, Yocto, Buildroot), as it already did in the updater contest. A proposed penalty for bootc's power-cut bug was **not** applied, because the updater contest scored the same bug at the same level. Applying it to bootc alone would break the "same standard for everyone" rule. It is shown as a variant: it alone would give Debian.
> 17. **What decides it next is already written down.** Canonical's answer (Z-01) and the bootc Pi 5 test (W2-HW-3). Pi 5 test fails → Debian. Canonical refuses → bootc. Both → too close to call, so it is your decision. Canonical agrees with a price → Ubuntu outright (§3.1.6 h). **Added after the refuters (FIX4):** Ubuntu's one-point lead comes from a Secure Boot score earned by a lab that ran **Debian**. So Ubuntu now gets its own Secure Boot test, in a virtual machine (no hardware to buy), before week 4. If it fails → bootc. Canonical's price page also shows what its permission most likely costs: "Trademark use" is sold inside Ubuntu Pro for Devices, a per-box subscription with no published price (§3.1.6 i).
> 18. **The update core got its own critics.** The health gate (DC-12) held, but "safe" now means three more things: hold app database migrations until the update commits; roll a multi-server cluster back through k3s's snapshot restore; and never let a starved app block a security update forever. The build-from-source question (DC-20) stays answered for a distro base, but because of NVIDIA support and one-person upkeep, not the CVE count.
> 19. **Arm and Secure Boot labs passed.** The update safety net passed on native Arm (RAUC 1.15.2 + k3s for arm64) [C-LAB-aarch64-gate-004], and Secure Boot passed again with RAUC 1.15.2 [C-LAB-secureboot-1152-005]. The "old signed kernel still boots" gap is still open [C-LAB-secureboot-1152-009].
>
> **What you must decide** (details in [`26-OPEN-QUESTIONS-FOR-ZEUS.md`](26-OPEN-QUESTIONS-FOR-ZEUS.md), Z-01…Z-27): ask counsel and Canonical about Ubuntu redistribution, now for Jetson too; accept or reject automatic security updates by default; RAUC (recommended), Mender or Rugix; who holds the signing keys; whether third parties may add host-level plugins in release 1. **Rev. 3 adds:** whether to start a CRA notified-body pre-assessment in parallel from 2027-Q1 (Z-05, §6.13), and run the Pi 5 test W2-HW-3 early, because it decides whether bootc's paper lead is real. **Rev. 4 adds:** Z-38 (accept the scorecard's base-OS result, Ubuntu, or override it as your own logged decision), Z-39 (does an unmet outside condition, like Canonical's permission, disqualify an option?), and Z-40 (write the tie-break counting rules down before the next tests).

**What did not change:** the update engine (RAUC; S2 tie-break 5–3), the health-gate design, k3s on platform nodes, restic, the §4.3 layout, the Jetson flavor, the kernel route at launch, DC-16's ten sockets. **What is still not done:** the rev-4 re-scores, recount and decision map now have three refuters (§5.1.2, FIX4; all *weakened*), but they were written one after another by one agent and are not blind (§12). The Ubuntu Secure Boot leg they ask for has not run. The loop is not dry.

## 2. Glossary

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **A/B update** | Two copies (slots) of the OS. Updates are written into the copy that isn't running; if the new copy fails, the box boots the old one. |
| **Health-gated commit** | The new slot becomes the default only after checks pass. Until then, any reboot returns to the old slot. |
| **Fresh signal** | Evidence produced *after this boot* (a new heartbeat, a local probe), as opposed to status saved in the database from an earlier boot. |
| **Rebase** | Moving a fielded box to a new major OS release (Debian 13 → 14) in place, without wiping it. |
| **Flavor** | A variant image for one hardware family (for example the Jetson flavor on NVIDIA's Ubuntu). |
| **Profile** | A configuration of the same image family for a use (greenhouse, TAK node, Industrial). |
| **bootc / image mode** | Shipping the whole OS as a container image; Red Hat's long-life lineage uses it. |
| **greenboot** | Fedora/RHEL health checks that trigger an automatic rollback. |
| **RAUC / Rugix / Mender** | Open-source A/B update engines (HAOS uses RAUC; umbrelOS uses Rugix). |
| **Verity bundle** | A RAUC update format whose every block is hash-checked, unlike the older "plain" format. |
| **k3s / RKE2 / k0s** | Small Kubernetes distributions. k3s and RKE2 come from SUSE; k0s from Mirantis. |
| **NATS leaf node** | A small NATS server on a device that forwards to the main bus and stores messages while the link is down. |
| **SQLite / etcd** | The two k3s databases: SQLite for one server, etcd for several. |
| **restic** | An encrypting, de-duplicating backup program that allows several keys per repository. |
| **CRA** | EU Cyber Resilience Act (Regulation (EU) 2024/2847); main obligations from 2027-12-11. |
| **Gate / anchor / tie** | Scorecard terms: a gate is pass/fail; an anchor is the written meaning of a 0–5 score; a gap under 3.0 points between the top two is a tie, settled by decision-critical evidence, never by changing scores. |
| **Refuter / steelman** | An independent agent told to disprove a conclusion / an agent told to argue for the runner-up. |
| **Weakened** | A refuter verdict meaning "the conclusion may stand but its evidence or scope must change". Every weakened verdict gets a written response in §5. |
| **UKI (unified kernel image)** | One signed EFI file holding the kernel, initrd and kernel command line, so the firmware checks all three at once. |
| **PCR 7 / PCR 11** | TPM registers. PCR 7 records the Secure Boot state and keys; PCR 11 records the booted UKI and boot phases. A disk key "bound" to them unlocks only when they match. |
| **SBAT** | A generation number inside signed boot files; raising it lets firmware or shim refuse older signed files (anti-downgrade). |
| **Platform / machine keyring** | Kernel key stores. Keys from UEFI db go to the *platform* keyring, which the kernel does not trust for modules; MOK keys go to the *machine* keyring, which it does [C-DEC-009, C-DEC-010]. |
| **MOK** | Machine Owner Key: a key added through shim, confirmed at the console at boot. |
| **sysext / confext** | systemd system and configuration extensions: signed images that lay files over `/usr` (sysext) or `/etc` (confext) on a read-only OS, and can be removed cleanly. |
| **Driver pack** | A sysext carrying kernel modules built and signed for one exact kernel. |
| **meta-tegra / OE4T** | The Yocto layer (and its community) that builds Jetson images; NVIDIA lists it as official since JetPack 7.2 [C-W7-008]. |
| **Revocation list freshness** | A signed "kill list" must expire and refuse older copies, or an attacker can hold a device on an old list (TUF's freeze and rollback attacks) [C-REF-W19-security-008]. |
| **Head-to-head (pairwise) tie-break** *(rev. 3; withdrawn in rev. 4)* | Comparing every pair of three candidates on the decision-critical rows. Rev. 3 added it after the scores were known and never logged it, so it was a protocol change, not part of the frozen rules [C-PROTO4-018]. On the rev-4 card it produces a cycle and no winner. |
| **Frozen-protocol result** *(rev. 4)* | What the rules locked in `scorecard-weights.md` produce when applied mechanically: gates, then weighted totals, then a tie (under 3.0 points) between **the top two** settled by the decision-critical rows. |
| **Rule-6 re-score** *(rev. 4)* | A cell change forced when a refuter shows a cell is inconsistent with its sources. It must be applied with the same standard to every candidate, and logged in `scorecard-weights.md` §8. |
| **Decision map (pre-registered)** *(rev. 4)* | A table, written before a test runs, saying which result each possible test outcome produces under the frozen rules. It stops the rules being bent after the answer is known. |
| **Swing row** *(rev. 4)* | A tie-break row whose reading alone can change a pair's winner. |
| **Trial boots / one-try** *(rev. 3)* | How many times a new OS version may boot before the box gives up on it. RAUC with HAOS's script allows 3; stock Mender allows 1, so a single power cut during that boot discards a good update [C-LAB-mender-013]. |
| **Production precedent** *(rev. 3)* | A shipped product, with constraints like OneMind's, that already runs the exact combination being recommended. Rule 2 makes a competitor pattern the default only then. |
| **dm-verity status line** *(rev. 3)* | A one-line status the Linux kernel reports for a verified block device. Linux 6.19 added a field to it, which older RAUC misreads, so RAUC 1.13 cannot install updates on newer kernels [C-LAB-ubuntu-2604-leg-003]. |

---

## 3. Scorecards

Formula: `total = Σ(weight × score)/5`, weights C1 20 · C2 20 · C3 15 · C4 15 · C5 10 · C6 10 · C7 10 (frozen v1.0, not changed by this file). Each scorecard below shows (1) gates, (2) the workstream's frozen scores with one claim per cell, (3) **the corrections this file makes, each forced by a refuter or by evidence that arrived after the workstream scored**, (4) sensitivity, (5) the tie-break where the top two are within 3 points. No weight or anchor was changed, so `scorecard-weights.md` §8 needs no entry; cell corrections are logged here per §5 rule 6 of that file. **Rev. 4 correction:** that sentence was wrong for revision 3, which changed the S1 finalist rule without a §8 row [C-PROTO4-016]. Revision 4 withdraws that rule and logs its own cell re-scores in §8 ("cells rev-4"). **Revision 3:** every cell now carries a claim, or sits at the floor, per the citation pass in [`evidence/scorecard-rev3.md`](evidence/scorecard-rev3.md) (logged in `scorecard-weights.md` §8 as "cells rev-3"). This file adopts its numbers and sensitivity runs unchanged; the cited per-cell IDs for S1 rows G/B/F/E and for S3–S5 are in that file's §3–§7.

|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

| C3 ±5 | D / D | A | 1.9 / 2.1 | | A / A | 1.9 / 2.1 |
| C4 ±5 | D / D | A | 1.9 / 2.1 | | A / A | 1.9 / 2.1 |
| C5 ±5 | D / D | A | 1.9 / 2.1 | | A / A | 1.9 / 2.1 |

**Withdrawn in rev. 4 (§3.1.6): the paragraph below applied a tie-break rule the frozen protocol does not contain.** **Tie-break verdict (rev. 3): A (Debian 13), kept.** Debian is the only finalist that no other finalist beats head-to-head: it beats Ubuntu 7–4 and draws with bootc 5–5, a draw it takes on the tier argument above; Ubuntu beats bootc 6–4. **The strict two-finalist reading** (C vs D only, because A is 4.0 behind C on the literal card) picks **D**, but D's G1 condition (Canonical's written permission, Z-01) is unmet and outside OneMind's control, so D cannot ship today; and A sits inside D's tie band and beats it head-to-head. Both readings therefore lead to the same action until Z-01 or W2-HW-3 changes an input. This is an interpretation of the frozen protocol (which names only "the top two"), stated openly, not a change of weights or anchors.

**What flips S1 in rev. 3 (each is pre-registered here):**
- **Toward C (bootc as the base):** W2-HW-3 passes (AlmaLinux bootc image on a real Pi 5, k3s-gated `bootc switch` to a broken image, automatic return with no console) **and** greenboot #219 (silent downgrade after a power cut in finalize) is fixed upstream [C-LAB-fedora-bootc-013] **and** a Pi update commit path exists that needs no manual `sync` [C-SCORE-026]. With all three, C wins the Pi 5 rows and leads A on the cited card outside the band.
- **Toward D (Ubuntu as the base):** unchanged from rev. 2 below: Z-01 answered favourably plus a priced Pro for Devices or the 26.04 Jetson kernel with CUDA. The Ubuntu CI leg (REC-DEC-17) is now proven runnable [C-LAB-ubuntu-2604-leg-011], so this switch is a build variable.
- **Weakens A further (not a flip on its own):** T-W3-04 measures a Debian major rebase above 4–6 founder-weeks (lifecycle-cost A-03), or a backports kernel ≥ 6.19 is needed on a launch board before OneMind's own RAUC package exists.

**Rev. 2 verdict, kept for the record. Tie-break verdict: A (Debian 13), kept, with less margin than revision 1.** Revision 2 moved four rows toward Ubuntu or to neutral (G1 narrowed, DC-03 unranked, DC-19 conditional, DC-17 tie) and added two Ubuntu rows (NVIDIA trajectory, IIoT vendor process). Debian still wins what is hard to undo and what we can prove: the core image ships without anyone's permission, it is the only base our labs ran (now four spikes, including Secure Boot and a real k3s gate), it has the constraint-matched precedent, and it has no single-vendor governance. Ubuntu's advantages sit on NVIDIA hardware and IIoT certification, and the flavors already capture the first.

**The cheap hedge, adopted now (REC-DEC-17):** build the core from one Dockerfile-style definition with **two CI legs, `FROM debian:trixie` and `FROM ubuntu:26.04`**, and run the lab suite against both on x86 (the Ubuntu steelman's "cheap step" [steelman-base-ubuntu §9]). The switch then becomes a variable, not a rewrite.

**What re-opens S1 (switch the core to Ubuntu 26.04):** revision 1 asked for all three of the items below. Revision 2 lowers this to **(1) plus either (2) or (3)**, because the Jetson flavor needs (1) anyway and the S1-8 variant shows (1) alone moves C6: (1) Canonical grants written redistribution permission for a modified OneMind image on acceptable terms (Q-W2-1), covering Jetson too; (2) Pro for Devices is priced within H-1 margins (Q168); (3) `linux-nvidia-tegra` 7.0 reaches the 26.04 updates pocket and runs CUDA on Orin and Thor in a founder test [C-STEEL-ubu-005], which turns D into one release family. LP #2170238 must also be fixed [C-STEEL-ubu-009]. **What re-opens it toward bootc:** a ledgered Pi 5 bootc result passing W2-HW-3 plus a fixed greenboot #219 downgrade bug [C-LAB-fedora-bootc-013].

#### 3.1.6 Revision 4: S1 under the frozen protocol (closes critic P3-1 and P3-2)

> **Explain it like I'm the founder.** We locked the contest rules before scoring. They say: if the top two are less than 3 points apart, it's a tie, and the tie is settled by the decision-critical evidence between **those two**. Revision 3 broke that rule. It brought Debian in as a third finalist after the scores were known, and Debian then won. Revision 4 takes that step back. It also applies two evidence corrections the refuters asked for, in the same way to every candidate. The result: **Ubuntu 65, bootc 64, Debian 63**. That is a tie between Ubuntu and bootc, and the locked tie-break gives it to **Ubuntu, 6 rows to 4**. It is a narrow, conditional win. Ubuntu still needs Canonical's permission (or a debranded rebuild) before OneMind can ship it, and two pending answers, Canonical's reply and the bootc Pi 5 test, could each change the winner. So we wrote down now, before either answer arrives, what each answer will mean.

**(a) What the frozen rules require.** `scorecard-weights.md` lines 1–195 are byte-identical in all three commits of this run. Line 67 says "a difference of under 3.0 points between the top two is a tie", settled by the decision-critical evidence and the refuter tables. Only a gate FAIL removes a candidate. A CONDITIONAL gate keeps the candidate ranked and sends its mitigation to 20 and 23. Changes need a §8 row, and only a factual anchor error or a new founder decision can justify one [C-PROTO4-001, C-PROTO4-002, C-PROTO4-003]. The full ruling is in [`evidence/adversarial/S1-protocol-ruling-rev4.md`](evidence/adversarial/S1-protocol-ruling-rev4.md).

**(b) The rev-3 three-way pairwise tie-break is withdrawn.** It changed who could win, it first appears in the commit that carries the rev-3 scores, it was not logged in §8, and neither allowed reason applies [C-PROTO4-007, C-PROTO4-018]. Three independent refuters reached the same finding on their own (S1R4 technical, longevity and security, §5.1.1), and so did the bootc steelman (§6.14). This file does not use the rule. It could return only as a founder decision logged in §8 before it is used (Z-38, Z-40). The rev-3 verdict "A (Debian 13), kept" (§3.1.5) is **withdrawn** and kept below only for the record. On the rev-3 card the two rules give: **frozen D > C > A** (tie C–D, tie-break D 6–4); **rev-3 pairwise A > D > C** [C-PROTO4-011]. Rev. 3 also set D aside because its G1 condition is unmet. The frozen §2 does not allow that: COND is ranked, and the same Canonical condition also sits on A's and C's Jetson flavor [C-PROTO4-002, C-PROTO4-013].

**(c) Rule-6 re-scores requested in rev. 4.** §5 rule 6 of the frozen file says a refuter finding that a cell is inconsistent with its sources forces a re-score. It also says every candidate gets the same evidence standard. Each request was judged against the anchor text and applied to every candidate it touches. The judgement was made before the totals were computed.

| # | Request (source) | Ruling | Reason |

Less weight on solo operability (C5) removes bootc's Containerfile edge. Less weight on ecosystem (C7) removes Ubuntu's ROS and NVIDIA edge. On raw score alone the leader also changes (C leads in 3 runs, A in 1), so no base wins on points.

**U-SB status (FIX5, 2026-11-11):** pre-registered in `evidence/lab/ubuntu-secureboot/` (README sha256 `c98a09d1…f8db`, frozen 15:51:12Z, using the U-SB row wording above as its rule) and attempted at once in a disposable VM. The result is **BLOCKED (environment)**: the Ubuntu 26.04 archive does not resolve from this research environment, while Debian, Fedora, kernel.org and GitHub do [C-FIX5-001]. FIX4 had already recorded the same block for ubuntu.com [C-FIX5-004]. **BLOCKED is not a map event:** no cell moves and the S1 result stays **D**, still resting on D·C3 = 4 marked `doc-only` [C-FIX5-002]. The kit is the `secureboot-1152` kit with only the pre-registered Ubuntu changes, and it has not yet been executed [C-FIX5-003]. It runs in about 1.5–2 hours, unattended, on any machine that can reach the Ubuntu archive, so it is scheduled for **week 1** (20, 22 LT-VM-20), ahead of W2-HW-3. The open question for the run owner is whether this environment may reach the Ubuntu archive or an approved mirror. The run did not pick a mirror itself.

**(i) What this file does with the result.** **Option (a) of the S1 ruling: the frozen-protocol result is the decision of record.** The case for Debian is real: no permission is needed for the core, umbrelOS is a constraint-matched precedent, and every lab ran on it. But in the protocol it shows up only as the A–D and A–C tie-break rows. The run does not turn it into a verdict. A founder who wants Debian now can choose it as his own decision, logged in §8 (Z-38 sets out the options). Rule 8 requires the run to state an assumption, so: **until Zeus rules, the run assumes the protocol result (D) with its COND mitigations.** Those mitigations, with owner and cost (frozen §2):
1. **G1 (Z-01):** send the drafted Canonical and counsel outreach in week 1 (owner: Zeus + counsel). RR-09 states no cost [C-DEC4-010]. The only bounded figure is DC-03's assumption of about $60 per box for Pro for Devices [C-COST-002], which `lifecycle-cost-2036.md` explicitly leaves out of the Canonical agreement. *FIX4:* Canonical's device pricing page lists "Trademark use" among the contents of **Ubuntu Pro for Devices**, which is sold per device, annually or once for up to 10 years, with no published price [C-FIX4-001, C-FIX4-003]. The likely mitigation is therefore a fee on every D box. **Provisional bracket (an assumption, not a quote):** $0 (fee waived) to $250 per box over 10 years, i.e. at or below the $25-per-year Ubuntu Pro workstation list price [C-FIX4-004]. DC-03's $60 sits inside it; a server-class price ($500 a year) would not. This bounds the COND only provisionally (critic P4-8): the decisive figure is Canonical's answer (Z-01), and Z-39 still decides how an uncosted COND counts. O-1 should also ask whether trademark use is sold only inside Pro for Devices, and whether it is tied to a Canonical kernel, since Canonical certification requires one [C-FIX4-002] (S1R5 longevity).
2. **Build both legs:** one image definition with `FROM ubuntu:26.04` and `FROM debian:trixie` legs, both lab-proven [C-LAB-ubuntu-2604-leg-011] (REC-DEC-17). The extra leg costs about 40 lines of bootstrap change plus CI time, so moving between A and D is a build variable, not a rewrite.
3. **Lock the shipping base at a named gate before the first box ships**, using the map in (h). A box shipped on one base cannot move to bootc without a reflash [C-STEEL4-bootc-025]. **W2-HW-3 therefore runs before gate G1 (week 4)** (REC-DEC-44).
4. **G5:** the Jetson flavor stays on NVIDIA's L4T Ubuntu 24.04 on every base, so this condition is the same for A, C and D.

Everything that came before the base choice in rev. 3 is unchanged and base-neutral between A and D: the §4.3 layout, OneMind's own RAUC, the fresh-signal gate, k3s, restic, the Jetson flavor and the K1 kernel route ("Ubuntu's kernel + Raspberry Pi's + NVIDIA's", §3.5 note). The Debian-specific text in §4.1 describes the A leg.

### 3.2 S2 · Image, A/B update and rollback framework (ADR-001 D3)

**Rev. 3 (scorecard-rev3 §4):** Mender 62.0 · RAUC 61.0 · bootc 58.0 (newly ranked; wins no sensitivity run, best 59.4 vs Mender 61.9) · Rugix 52.0. SWUpdate's C2 drops 2 → 1 and it stays unranked (G2 FAIL). **No rev. 3 lab result moves an S2 cell:** Mender's lab pass confirms anchor 3 for its C3 (it has no verity bundles and no revocation, so anchor 4 stays out of reach [C-STEEL-updates-mender-023]); RAUC 1.15.2 repeats RAUC's existing C3 = 4 evidence.

**Revision 2 corrections (each forced by new evidence or by applying one standard to both engines):**
- **S2-2, RAUC C3 3 → 4.** Revision 1 lowered it because anchor 4's "Secure Boot path with no later bootloader or partition change" was a design inference. Spike X-2R now shows it: Debian's unpatched RAUC 1.13 `efi` backend ran A/B, rollback and firmware refusal of tampered and unsigned UKIs under enforced Secure Boot on the §4.3 layout, unchanged [C-LAB-secureboot-rauc-012…015, C-LAB-secureboot-rauc-020]. Key rotation and revocation exist in the engine (CRL, signer allow-list) [C-W3-018] even though HAOS does not use CRLs. Anchor 5 still needs a dm-verity root (not built), so 4 is the ceiling. This is emulation, which anchor 4 allows ("documented"), so no `pending-lab` mark is needed.
- **S2-3, Mender C6 3 → 4.** The Mender steelman showed that C6 was scored on a missing feature (paid deltas), while anchor 4 reads only "free for all units, permissive or weak-copyleft licenses". Mender OSS server and client are Apache-2.0 and free at any fleet size [C-STEEL-updates-mender-001]. RAUC got 4 on the same reading. Paid deltas stay a **tie-break** argument (below), counted once.
- **S2-4, Mender C4 2 → 3.** Anchor 3 accepts "one product whose constraints match OneMind's". umbrelOS, the constraint-matched appliance, shipped on Mender, and its current code still builds a `umbrelos-mender-amd64` variant for those devices [C-W3-040]. Leaving it is not un-shipping it.
- **Rejected:** the Rugix steelman's C5 3 → 4 and C7 2 → 3 (its own file calls C7 "contested"; C-W3-016 is SECONDARY, so the protocol caps the cell). They are shown as a variant only. Even with them, Rugix (56.0) is 5–6 points behind.

**Correction S2-1 (RAUC C3 4 → 3):** both refuters on P0-W3-2 showed the anchor-4 items are weaker than scored. RAUC's CRL support is not used by its reference adopter: HAOS's 2026 PKI rotation dropped CRLs from its keyrings [C-TD-home-assistant-os-014]; RAUC had a 2026 signature-coverage CVE for large "plain" bundles [C-DEC-004]; and RAUC has no native systemd-boot backend [C-DEC-001], so the "Secure Boot without a later layout change" item is a design inference until spike X-2R. Anchor 5 is not reachable either way (no dm-verity root yet). Lab results lift nothing above 3 on their own, but they do confirm anchor 3 *in our lab* [C-LAB-rauc-015…018].

**Sensitivity (rev. 1):** RAUC 58.0 vs Mender 57.0, a tie; Mender won 3 of 14 runs.
**Sensitivity (rev. 2):** **Mender 62.0 vs RAUC 61.0. Mender leads in 12 of 14 runs and RAUC in 2 (C2 −5, C3 +5); the largest margin is 2.2, so all 14 runs are inside the 3-point tie band.** Under the frozen protocol this is a tie, and the tie-break decides, not the raw order. **Fragile, and the raw order has flipped since revision 1.**

**Tie-break (decision-critical evidence), re-run for revision 2, then recounted for revision 3** (rev. 3 changes are marked; the rows below the list are rev. 2 text unless marked):
- **DC-18 (Secure Boot later without a layout change):** RAUC is lab-proven on this layout under enforced Secure Boot [C-LAB-secureboot-rauc-012…016]. Mender's Secure Boot route through mender-convert is x86 UEFI only, and its docs say Secure Boot "does not provide filesystem integrity" [C-STEEL-updates-mender-014]. → **RAUC.**
- **Security model (C3, R-1):** RAUC has verity bundles, CRL and signer allow-lists; Mender verifies against a key list, with no revocation [C-STEEL-updates-mender-023]. → **RAUC.**
- **Cost:** Mender OSS has no deltas, phased rollouts, RBAC or Gateway; deltas start at the paid Professional tier [C-STEEL-updates-mender-015]. RAUC's adaptive updates are free [C-W3-019]. For off-grid links (T-W3-05) that is a real product gap. → **RAUC.**
- **Pi 5:** RAUC uses Raspberry Pi's native `tryboot` (HAOS) [C-W3-029]; Mender's Pi 5 configs [C-STEEL-updates-mender-017] go through U-Boot, whose robustness under Pi 5 EEPROM updates is unverified (Mender steelman §7 item 5). → **RAUC.** **Rev. 3, narrowed:** neither engine has run on a Pi 5. RAUC's custom tryboot backend, built from 1.15.2, passed a desk pre-flight on the real Raspberry Pi OS tree that also found and fixed a "reports success without arming tryboot" bug [C-LAB-rauc-1152-017 (partial: desk pre-flight, no Pi 5 boot)]; the Pi 5 firmware falls back after **one** missed tryboot, so on Pi 5 RAUC also gets one trial boot [C-LAB-rauc-1152-012]. → **RAUC, narrowly** (documented native path vs an unverified U-Boot path); founder test F-PI5.
- **Lab proof (rev. 3: dropped, per N7):** rev. 2 counted this for RAUC because "Mender has none". Mender then passed the **same** pre-registered battery with the same unchanged gate, layout, kernel and k3s, in two runs [C-LAB-mender-008…014], except T9 (below). → **Neutral; removed from the count.**
- **Boot tries (rev. 3, new, from the Mender lab):** stock Mender's GRUB logic allows exactly **one** trial boot, so a power cut or transient hang during the first boot of a good update discards it (T9 failed in both runs, as predicted) [C-LAB-mender-003, C-LAB-mender-013]; RAUC with HAOS's script retries up to 3 times and committed the same update [C-LAB-rauc-k3s-gate-010, C-LAB-rauc-1152-011]. §4.2 item 3 requires 3 tries. A 3-try patch to Mender's boot script looks small but is **untested** (Mender lab F3) and would be a OneMind-maintained fork for GRUB and U-Boot. → **RAUC (x86), until F3 passes.**
- **Integration effort (rev. 3, new):** Mender needs no OneMind trial marker (its env flips only after the whole write) and reverts the env on a refused commit [C-LAB-mender-001], where RAUC needed a pre-install marker and explicit `mark-bad` [C-LAB-rauc-k3s-gate-001]; but a Mender gate placed in the root filesystem is **silently skipped** and every update commits unchecked, so the gate must ride inside every artifact [C-LAB-mender-007]. → **Neutral.**
- **Proven competitor on a Debian base (rev. 3, new, §9.1):** no field-proven product runs Debian or Ubuntu with RAUC [C-DEC-013, UNVERIFIED: negative search]; the only public Debian 13 + RAUC appliance OS found, IronFlockOS, is pre-1.0 [C-CIT4-001]; umbrelOS, the constraint-matched Debian appliance, shipped on Mender before Rugix and still builds a Mender variant for those devices [C-W3-040]. RAUC's appliance precedents (HAOS, ZimaOS) are Buildroot-based [C-TD-casaos-zimaos-008]. → **Mender** (for the Debian combination; RAUC keeps the stronger engine-level record).
- **DC-09 (Jetson), rev. 1 counted for RAUC, now neutral or Mender.** On stock L4T neither engine is native. Mender has a published Update Module that wraps NVIDIA OTA, but it is a community recipe with unanswered failure reports [C-STEEL-updates-mender-012 (SECONDARY: community tutorial)]. RAUC's Tegra layer was removed in 2024 [C-W3-021, C-STEEL-jb-021]. If the Jetson flavor moves to meta-tegra (§4.1), meta-tegra's redundant-rootfs path names swupdate, rauc and mender [C-STEEL-jb-020], but only Mender has a maintained Tegra integration [C-W3-053]. → **neutral today; Mender if meta-tegra wins.**
- **F-11 breadth:** Mender alone spans Zephyr MCUs (mender-mcu) to Linux with one artifact format and one self-hostable Apache-2.0 server [C-STEEL-updates-mender-005, C-STEEL-updates-mender-006]. RAUC has no server; OneMind must supply the fleet plane. → **Mender.**
- **DC-06 (maintainers):** Rugix's bus factor was upheld by all three refuters [C-W3-001, C-REF-W3-longevity-002]. **Wording corrected (Tier A D3 refuter):** RAUC is maintained by Pengutronix, mainly by two engineers (86% of commits), with a 10-year record and large adopters (HAOS in code; Steam Deck and IKEA Dirigera per the vendor) [C-REF-W3-longevity-001, C-REF-W3-longevity-007]; Mender's server had 22 human authors in a year [C-STEEL-updates-mender-004]. Neither RAUC nor Rugix publishes support beyond the latest release [C-REF-W3-longevity-008]. → **Mender ≥ RAUC > Rugix.**

**Verdict (rev. 3, final for this run): RAUC still leads, on the tie-break, now 5 rows to 3** (RAUC: DC-18, security model, cost, Pi 5 narrowly, boot tries · Mender: F-11 breadth, maintainers, Debian precedent · neutral: lab, integration effort, DC-09). If Mender's F3 (3-try boot script) passes, the boot-tries row goes neutral and the count is **4–3**, still RAUC. **Conditions, rev. 3:** OneMind ships **its own RAUC built from a pinned upstream tag** (today v1.15.2 at `4fb7c798`), because Debian 13's RAUC cannot install any bundle on a kernel ≥ 6.19 [C-LAB-ubuntu-2604-leg-010, C-LAB-ubuntu-2604-leg-004] and Debian stable will not follow upstream releases, which the 30-day rule needs; the version floor is "upstream ≥ 1.15.2, or Debian ≥ 1.13-3+deb13u1 on a ≤ 6.18 kernel only" [C-LAB-rauc-1152-005]; CI boots the newest kernel a product may run and runs T1–T3. **What flips S2 to Mender (rev. 3):** F3 passes **and** E3 (stock-L4T Jetson) passes, **and** either the Jetson flavor moves to meta-tegra or a free delta path is measured on the T-W3-05 link. Mender's lab pass removed E2 from this list.

**Verdict (rev. 2): RAUC still leads, on the tie-break (5 rows to 2, one neutral), not on the raw score.** Conditions are unchanged (verity bundles only, version ≥ 1.15.2 or a confirmed backport, 3 boot tries, engine-neutral layout; §4.2), plus one new rule: **track RAUC's latest release within 30 days**, because neither RAUC nor Debian backports security fixes on a published window [C-REF-W3-longevity-008, C-LAB-rauc-k3s-gate-005]. **Mender is the named runner-up**: its lab spike (Mender steelman E2) and the stock-L4T Update Module test (E3, folded into T-W3-06) are added to the 180-day plan. **What flips S2 to Mender:** E2 and E3 pass, **and** either the Jetson flavor moves to meta-tegra or a free delta path for Mender is measured on the T-W3-05 link. **Rugix remains a legitimate founder choice** (it passed our lab on both architectures with a real k3s gate [C-LAB-debian-rugix-022]); its flip conditions are in §6.8.

### 3.3 S3 · App runtime and Kubernetes (ADR-001 D4)

**Gates (W4, parts S3 controls):** k3s passes all with G3/G4 CONDITIONAL [C-W4-001 in 05, C-W4-012]. RKE2 G5 CONDITIONAL (4 GB minimum, 8 GB recommended) [C-W4-072 in 05]. MicroShift FAIL G2. **Docker/Compose, Podman+Quadlet and the tiered mix FAIL G5** (no single app platform from Pi to a multi-node GPU cluster) [C-W4-043, C-W4-044].

(Full cells in `05-…` §2.2; **rev. 3** citations for every cell, including RKE2 C6 [C-LIC-033] and Talos [C-TD-talos-012, C-TD-talos-040], are in scorecard-rev3 §5. MicroK8s leaves the ranking: G5 PASS → FAIL, no small-node footprint source [C-SCORE-008]. The tiered mix and Podman drop to 52.0 and 49.0. Compose's 64.0 survives full citation; a stricter C1 = 2 gives 60.0.)

**Corrections:** none to the numbers. The refuters forced two changes to the **decision text**, not the scores: (1) the platform-node gate must include **storage**, not only RAM: k3s's own docs say SD cards and eMMC "cannot handle the IO load" [C-W4-002]; (2) the competitor framing must include TrueNAS SCALE, a single-box appliance that ran its app catalog on Kubernetes and then moved it to Docker in 24.10 [C-DEC-007], for app-portability and user-complexity reasons (refuter W4-P0-1-technical).

**Revision 2 (security refuters, text only):** (3) the footprint gate was measured on k3s with default flags. The CIS hardening profile adds audit logging and other work [C-REF-W4-security-004], so the 4 GB + SSD gate must be re-measured on the hardened profile (founder test on Pi 5 NVMe; T-W4 soak). (4) k3s has had insecure-default advisories [C-REF-W4-security-001, C-REF-W4-security-003] and a path-traversal bug in snapshot restore [C-REF-W4-security-002], so every node runs a pinned OneMind k3s config, never upstream defaults, and restores accept only OneMind-signed snapshots. (5) **The gate set has no compliance gate.** With a FIPS gate, k3s would fail it and RKE2 (FIPS 140-3 certificate #4968, Canal CNI only [C-REF-W4-security-006, C-REF-W4-security-007]) would pass. This file keeps k3s as the default because no buyer requires FIPS before the regulated tier is sold; if the founder makes compliance a P0 product property (Z-25), the tie breaks to RKE2 on nodes of 8 GB or more.

**Sensitivity (W4):** **fragile**: the winner changes to RKE2 in 3 of 14 runs [05 §2.3]. **Tie-break:** k3s and RKE2 are one family (same vendor, snapshot CLI, upgrade controller) [C-W4-012], so the flip means "use the bigger sibling on GPU servers", which is the recommendation. Talos as an S3 *runtime* is ranked (61.0, G2 CONDITIONAL in 05 §2.1: Pi 5 overlay plus an open Orin PR as a "separate flavor"); Talos as an S1 *base OS* is excluded by S1's G2 (no merged Orin support) [C-W4-064]. Rev. 3 states both, resolving the inconsistency scorecard-rev3 §13 flagged. k0s rising to 60.0 does not change the order; it is still at or below k3s on every criterion and stays the named alternative in the bake-off. **Gates relaxed:** with G5 relaxed, Compose (64) ties k3s, so **G5 (F-14) is the only gate keeping Compose out**; §6 summarises its steelman.

**Revision 2 (Kopia steelman):** the Kopia C1 cell in 06 cites C-W5-030, which is the borg claim; the steelman supplies Kopia sources [C-STEEL-backup-kopia-014…016] at the same score (3). It argues C3 3 → 4 (Kopia sets and renews S3/Azure/GCS object locks itself; restic's extra keys cannot revoke a leaked key either [C-STEEL-backup-kopia-004, C-STEEL-backup-kopia-005]) and C7 3 → 4 (Velero and Kasten use Kopia as their data mover). Accepting both gives **restic 75 vs Kopia 71**: restic still wins every run, but the margin is 4, not 9. This file accepts the citation fix and shows the 71 as a variant. **What flips S4:** a two-engine Pi 5 4 GB memory test with a 1 TB repository (T-W5-01 run for both) in which restic exceeds 1 GB or is OOM-killed and Kopia stays under (Kopia 75 vs restic 71), or a founder decision that object-locked S3 is the default ransomware control. Kopia's own weaknesses keep it second: badly wrong clocks can make it delete its own data, a real risk on RTC-less off-grid boxes [C-STEEL-backup-kopia-010]; it promises to read only the previous minor format [C-STEEL-backup-kopia-011]; and VolSync has no Kopia mover.
